| 62b7639a5a68:19 | This skill works out which country taxes the pay of a person who lives on one side of the German-Swiss border and works on the other, and how Germany taxes the result. It also lists the German rules that follow a German who moves to Switzerland. It works from the treaty of 11 August 1971 as amended by the protocol of 21 August 2023. That protocol came into force on 27 November 2025 and applies from 1 January 2026. | Exact wording retained at currentLF19, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:21 | See also, and do not repeat here: | Exact wording retained at currentLF21, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:23 | - **ch-cross-border** (Switzerland pack): Swiss residence, tax at source, the CHF 120,000 ordinary assessment threshold, lump-sum taxation, Swiss withholding tax refunds. | Exact wording retained at currentLF23, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:24 | - **leaving-germany-exit-tax**: the § 6 AStG exit tax on shares and the § 2 AStG extended limited liability, with the full method. | Exact wording retained at currentLF24, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:25 | - **de-non-resident-tax**: a person who has left Germany and still has German income. | Exact wording retained at currentLF25, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:26 | - **de-partners-and-part-year**: the year of moving out, with the progression clause. | Exact wording retained at currentLF26, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:27 | - **de-employee-income-tax**: the 2026 tariff, Werbungskosten and special expenses of an employee. | Exact wording retained at currentLF27, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:28 | - **de-treaty-withholding-rates**: German withholding on dividends, interest and royalties paid to Swiss residents. | Exact wording retained at currentLF28, Germany and Switzerland: frontier commuters, weekly stayers and movers, tax year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:32 | Three groups, with different results. Classify the client first and test again after any change of job, home office pattern, flat or family home. | Exact wording retained at currentLF32, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:34 | **1. Frontier commuter (Grenzgänger), art. 15a.** A person resident in one state whose place of work is in the other state and who regularly returns from there to their home. The pay is taxed in the state of residence. The work state may withhold tax, capped at 4.5% of gross pay if the person shows an official residence certificate from their home tax office (art. 15a(1)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF34, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:36 | - **Place of work** is normally where the employee is integrated into the employer's business. For drivers and field staff, days away count as business trips when counting non-return days. Where the contract names several sites or none, the place of work is where the work is mostly done (protocol no. 5(a)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF36, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:37 | - **Regular return, new from 2026.** There is a regular return only if the employee travels from home to the place of work and back on at least 20% of the agreed working days in the calendar year (protocol no. 5(b)). Agreed working days are those in the contract. Protocol no. 5(d) takes leave and sick days out of the working days for the 60-day test; the protocol does not say whether they also come out of the base for the 20% test, so check both bases (Example 2 passes and fails the same way on either). | Exact wording retained at currentLF37, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:38 | - **The 60 non-return days test.** A person who does not return home after every working day keeps commuter status unless, when employed for the whole calendar year, they fail to return home on more than 60 working days because of their work (art. 15a(2)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF38, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:39 | - **What counts as a non-return day.** A day on which returning home was not possible or not reasonable for work reasons, and the person in fact did not return. Return is not reasonable when the shortest road distance one way is over 100 km by car, or the fastest public transport one way at usual commuting times takes over 1.5 hours; the means of transport mostly used decides (protocol no. 5(e), which takes over the consultation agreement of 12 October 2018). One-day business trips in either treaty state are not non-return days; one-day trips to a third country always are (protocol no. 5(f)). Weekend and holiday days on a multi-day business trip paid by the employer, and days off worked on the employer's express order, are working days (protocol no. 5(d)). Shift work spread over several days does not break the regular return; breaks under four hours do not end the working day (protocol no. 5(c)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF39, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:40 | - **Home office days.** Under the consultation agreement of 15 and 18 July 2022 (BMF letter of 26 July 2022), a working day spent entirely at home in the residence state is not a non-return day. That agreement was made under the treaty as amended in 2010; whether it still applies under the 2026 protocol is not confirmed (see the end of this skill). Either way, a home office day is not a day on which the person travels to the place of work and back, so from 2026 it counts against the 20% regular-return test. | Exact wording retained at currentLF40, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:41 | - **Part-year employment.** The allowed non-return days are 5 per full month and 1 per full week of employment in the other state (protocol no. 5(g)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF41, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:42 | - **Part-time employment.** Someone who works some hours on every usual working day keeps the 60 days. Someone who works only on some days gets 60 cut in proportion: contractual working days over the usual full-time working days, taken as 240 for a five-day week and 280 for a six-day week if the contract is silent. Leave is not deducted from either figure (protocol no. 5(i)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF42, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:43 | - **Several employers in the work state.** Status is judged once for all jobs in the year; non-return days from all jobs are added (protocol no. 5(h)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF43, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:45 | The rule works both ways. For a Swiss resident who commutes to a German employer, the German employer withholds at most 4.5% and Switzerland taxes the pay, cutting the gross pay by one fifth when computing its tax base (art. 15a(3)(b)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF45, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:47 | **2. Weekly stayer (Wochenaufenthalter) and others who lose commuter status.** A German resident with a weekday room in Switzerland who stays there on more than 60 working days is not a commuter. Art. 15 applies instead: Switzerland may tax pay for work done in Switzerland. Germany exempts that pay but uses it to set the rate on other income (art. 24(1) no. 1(c) and no. 2; § 32b(1) no. 3 EStG). Germany grants the exemption only if the person proves that Switzerland taxed the pay and the tax was paid, or that Switzerland waived its right (§ 50d(8) EStG). Pay for workdays spent outside Switzerland stays taxable in Germany. | Exact wording retained at currentLF47, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:49 | A weekday flat does not by itself move the person's residence. If the person is resident in both states under domestic law, art. 4(2) decides: permanent home first, then centre of vital interests (closer personal and economic ties), then habitual abode, then nationality. A family home in Germany usually points to Germany. | Exact wording retained at currentLF49, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:51 | **3. Managers of Swiss companies, art. 15(4).** A German resident who is a board member (Vorstandsmitglied), director, managing director (Geschäftsführer) or Prokurist of a Swiss company can be taxed in Switzerland on that pay, unless their duties are limited to work outside Switzerland. Art. 15a takes priority, so a manager who meets the commuter test is a commuter. The German and Swiss authorities agreed on 6 April 2023 to apply art. 15(4) also to people registered with a signature right and no stated function, and to unregistered people with powers at least equal to a Prokura. That agreement now runs to 31 December 2027 (BMF letters of 25 April 2023 and 16 October 2025). | Exact wording retained at currentLF51, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:53 | **4. Germans who move to Switzerland.** Moving ends German unlimited liability. Four German rules can follow: | Exact wording retained at currentLF53, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:55 | - **Treaty tail, art. 4(4).** A Swiss resident without Swiss nationality who was fully taxable in Germany for at least five years in total remains taxable in Germany on German-source income in the year their German liability ended and the five following years. Germany credits the Swiss tax on that income. This does not apply to someone who moved to take up genuine employment with an employer in which they have no substantial interest. A German employer may not apply the 4.5% cap to such a person while art. 4(4) applies (Gre-1 note 3). | Exact wording retained at currentLF55, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:56 | - **Exit tax, § 6 AStG.** Shares of at least 1% held in the last five years, after at least seven years of full German liability in the last twelve: deemed sale on leaving, seven yearly instalments on application. Method in leaving-germany-exit-tax. | Exact wording retained at currentLF56, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:57 | - **Extended limited liability, § 2 AStG.** A German citizen who moves to a low-tax country and keeps substantial German economic interests stays taxable on more German income for ten years. Whether the Swiss canton counts as low-tax is a computation; see leaving-germany-exit-tax. | Exact wording retained at currentLF57, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:58 | - **Gift and inheritance tax, § 2(1) no. 1(b) ErbStG.** A German national who has lived abroad for no more than five years without a German home still counts as a resident for gift and inheritance tax. | Exact wording retained at currentLF58, Who this applies to |
| 62b7639a5a68:60 | **Lump-sum taxpayers, art. 4(6).** A person is not treated as resident in a treaty state if that state does not tax them, at the generally applied taxes, on all their income from the other state that is normally taxable there. A lump-sum (expenditure-based) taxpayer in Switzerland who is not taxed on German-source income at ordinary rates therefore loses treaty protection. The Swiss rules on the lump sum are in ch-cross-border. | Exact wording retained at currentLF60, Who this applies to |

| 62b7639a5a68:90 | 5. If both tests pass, art. 15a applies to the whole year's pay from that employment. If either fails, go to Step 3. | Exact wording retained at currentLF90, Step 1: is the person a commuter? |
| 62b7639a5a68:92 | The employer certifies the non-return days on form Gre-3, stamped by the tax office for the place of work (protocol no. 5(j)). Where the employer finds at the year end, or when the job ends, that commuter status fails because of the non-return days, it sends the Gre-3 to the tax authority that collects the withholding, if possible within three months (Gre-3 note 2). The employee must give the employer the facts needed (protocol no. 5(j)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF92, Step 1: is the person a commuter? |
| 62b7639a5a68:96 | - All the pay, including pay for days worked in Switzerland, is German employment income. Compute it as for any employee (de-employee-income-tax): gross pay less Werbungskosten or the employee lump sum, then special expenses. | Exact wording retained at currentLF96, Step 2: the German assessment of a commuter (German resident) |
| 62b7639a5a68:97 | - The 2026 tariff applies to taxable income, with the solidarity surcharge at 5.5% above the EUR 20,350 threshold of income tax (see de-employee-income-tax). | Exact wording retained at currentLF97, Step 2: the German assessment of a commuter (German resident) |
| 62b7639a5a68:98 | - The Swiss tax withheld, up to 4.5% of gross, is credited against German income tax as if it were German withholding (art. 15a(3)(a); § 36(2) no. 2 EStG). It is not capped under § 34c. The employer's certificate of gross pay and Swiss tax is the evidence (protocol no. 6). | Exact wording retained at currentLF98, Step 2: the German assessment of a commuter (German resident) |
| 62b7639a5a68:99 | - The Swiss employer withholds the lower of 4.5% and the Swiss tariff tax (Gre-1 note 6(b)). The commuter gives the employer the Gre-1 when starting work and when changing employer; the German tax office then renews it each year without a new application (Gre-1 notes 2 and 7). | Exact wording retained at currentLF99, Step 2: the German assessment of a commuter (German resident) |
| 62b7639a5a68:103 | - Pay for work days in Switzerland: Swiss tax under art. 15; exempt in Germany with progression (art. 24(1) no. 1(c), no. 2; § 32b EStG), only with proof of Swiss tax paid (§ 50d(8)). | Exact wording retained at currentLF103, Step 3: when commuter status fails |
| 62b7639a5a68:104 | - Pay for days worked in Germany or third countries: taxable in Germany. | Exact wording retained at currentLF104, Step 3: when commuter status fails |
| 62b7639a5a68:105 | - If the employer finds during the year that status will fail, it must collect the full Swiss tax (Gre-1 note 4). | Exact wording retained at currentLF105, Step 3: when commuter status fails |
| 62b7639a5a68:109 | A former commuter who worked in the Swiss public service and draws a second-pillar pension keeps the Germany-first rule: the Swiss pension fund withholds at most 4.5% on a Gre-4 certificate (Gre-5 for survivors), and Germany credits it in proportion to the taxable share of the pension (BMF letters of 8 February 2018 and 7 December 2023; protocol no. 9). | Exact wording retained at currentLF109, Step 4: Swiss occupational pensions of former commuters |

Due 31 July 2027, a Saturday, so Monday 2 August 2027; with a tax adviser 29 February 2028 (§§ 108, 149 AO; see de-non-resident-tax) \| | Exact wording retained at currentLF117, Deadlines and filing |

| 62b7639a5a68:123 | - **Late filing surcharge (§ 152 AO).** 0.25% per started month of the assessed tax less prepayments and credited withholding, at least EUR 25 per month, at most EUR 25,000. Compulsory if the 2026 return is not filed by 29 February 2028 and tax is due. | Exact wording retained at currentLF123, Penalties and interest |
| 62b7639a5a68:124 | - **Interest on assessed tax (§ 233a, § 238 AO).** 0.15% per full month on the balance, from 1 April 2028 for 2026. | Exact wording retained at currentLF124, Penalties and interest |
| 62b7639a5a68:125 | - **Late payment (§ 240 AO).** 1% per started month on overdue tax, rounded down to the nearest EUR 50. | Exact wording retained at currentLF125, Penalties and interest |
| 62b7639a5a68:126 | - **Lost commuter status found late.** The employer must collect the Swiss tax not withheld (Gre-1 note 4). In Germany the exemption under art. 24 is refused until the Swiss tax is shown paid (§ 50d(8) EStG), so the return is assessed with the pay taxed and changed later. | Exact wording retained at currentLF126, Penalties and interest |
| 62b7639a5a68:128 | Figures and sections as in de-non-resident-tax, which read them on 2026-10-23. | Exact wording retained at currentLF128, Penalties and interest |
| 62b7639a5a68:132 | - **Two treaty layers from 2026.** The protocol of 21 August 2023 rewrote the commuter points into the treaty protocol (nos. 5 to 7) and ended the 1991 negotiation record (protocol no. 7). The 20% regular-return test is the main new condition. | Exact wording retained at currentLF132, Cross-border points |
| 62b7639a5a68:133 | - **Swiss withholding for German residents, dividends, interest.** Switzerland withholds 35% Verrechnungssteuer; under art. 10 a German resident can recover down to 15% (0% for a German company holding at least 10% for 365 days); interest is taxable only in Germany (art. 11). Refund route in ch-cross-border. | Exact wording retained at currentLF133, Cross-border points |
| 62b7639a5a68:134 | - **German withholding on payments to Swiss residents.** See de-treaty-withholding-rates. | Exact wording retained at currentLF134, Cross-border points |
| 62b7639a5a68:135 | - **Social security.** EU-Switzerland coordination decides where contributions are due, separately from tax. Not covered here. | Exact wording retained at currentLF135, Cross-border points |
| 62b7639a5a68:136 | - **Inheritance tax.** A separate Germany-Switzerland inheritance tax treaty of 1978 exists. Not covered here. | Exact wording retained at currentLF136, Cross-border points |
| 62b7639a5a68:137 | - **Currency.** Swiss pay is converted to euro for the German return. The conversion rule is not set out here. | Exact wording retained at currentLF137, Cross-border points |
| 62b7639a5a68:143 | Lena lives in Lörrach and works full time for a Basel employer all of 2026. She commutes every working day except 12 nights away on multi-day business trips in Switzerland and 20 home office days at home. She is single and has no other income. Her gross pay is EUR 80,000 (already converted). After Werbungskosten and special expenses her taxable income is EUR 60,000 (computed as in de-employee-income-tax). She gave her employer a Gre-1. (Invented case.) | Exact wording retained at currentLF143, Example 1: commuter from Lörrach to Basel, full year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:145 | - Non-return days: 12, below 60. Home office days do not count. | Exact wording retained at currentLF145, Example 1: commuter from Lörrach to Basel, full year 2026 |
| 62b7639a5a68:146 | - Regular return: she commutes on almost every working day, far above 20%. | Exact wording retained at currentLF146, Example 1: commuter from Lörrach to Basel, full year 2026 |

| 62b7639a5a68:148 | - German tax on EUR 60,000: z = (60,000 − 17,799) / 10,000 = 4.2201; tax = (173.10 × 4.2201 + 2,397) × 4.2201 + 1,034.87 = 14,233.23, rounded down EUR 14,233. | 62b7639a5a68:173 | Jonas lives in Konstanz and works three days a week for a Zurich employer all of 2026. His contract does not state the usual full-time days, so a five-day week gives 240. His contractual days are 3/5 of 240 = 144. He takes 12 days of leave, so he has 132 working days. He works 30 of them at home in Konstanz and stays overnight in Switzerland on 30 days because of evening client work more than 100 km from home. (Invented case.) | Exact wording retained at currentLF173, Example 2: part-time commuter with home office and overnight trips |
| 62b7639a5a68:175 | - Non-return limit: 60 × 144 / 240 = 36 days (leave is not deducted for this ratio). His 30 days are within it. | Exact wording retained at currentLF175, Example 2: part-time commuter with home office and overnight trips |
| 62b7639a5a68:176 | - Regular return: commuting days = 132 − 30 − 30 = 72. 20% of 132 = 26.4. 72 is above, so he passes. On the contract days without deducting leave (144) the threshold is 28.8; he still passes. | Exact wording retained at currentLF176, Example 2: part-time commuter with home office and overnight trips |
| 62b7639a5a68:177 | - He is a commuter: Switzerland withholds at most 4.5% and Germany taxes the pay and credits it, as in Example 1. | Exact wording retained at currentLF177, Example 2: part-time commuter with home office and overnight trips |
| 62b7639a5a68:178 | - Edge case: if he had worked 80 days at home, his commuting days would be 132 − 80 − 30 = 22, below 26.4 (and below 28.8). He would then fail the regular-return test from 2026 even though the home office days are not non-return days. Art. 15 would then apply: Switzerland taxes pay for his Swiss work days and Germany exempts it with progression, on proof of Swiss tax. | Exact wording retained at currentLF178, Example 2: part-time commuter with home office and overnight trips |
